Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… the main reason I came in is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s gotten really hard to finish even normal work tasks. Like, I’m a software developer, and I’ll be fixing some rendering bug—something that should just be me following the logic, checking the component, reading the stack trace—and then a Slack notification pops up, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain is off on a completely different track.

And it doesn’t feel like ordinary distraction, exactly. It’s like each little thing pulls me into a whole new idea before I’ve finished the line of code I was writing. I’ll realize I’ve abandoned the original problem and I’m thinking about redesigning some unrelated UI thing, or looking into why the siren sounded, or following a thought that felt important for a second. Then I look back and I’ve got half-finished code and I’m not even sure where I was going with it. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s really interfering with my ability to stay on track.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it’s not just at work. Work is where it’s most obvious because there’s a task in front of me and I can actually see the trail of unfinished things, but at home it happens too.

Like, I’ll go into the kitchen to do something simple—make coffee, grab a glass, whatever—and then I’ll notice a reflection or a pattern of light on the counter, or my phone buzzes, or I hear something outside, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing. And then a few minutes later I’m standing there with the cabinet open and I don’t even remember exactly what I came in for.

It feels like the same thing as when I’m coding, honestly. I’ll be in the middle of one thought, and then some tiny thing pulls me into a whole other chain of ideas before I finish the first one. I keep thinking, “Okay, just stay with the task,” but it’s like my attention keeps getting yanked sideways.

Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. There have been times where I’ll get this feeling that if I don’t move right now, the whole thing is going to fall apart or the opportunity will be gone, and then I just… act.

The clearest example is I spent money my wife and I hadn’t agreed on. It wasn’t like buying a random toy or something—it made sense to me at the time, very much. I bought GPUs, and printing materials, and paid for some promo video stuff because I was convinced the project needed to scale immediately and look professional immediately. In my head it wasn’t “spending,” it was more like, “If I don’t secure this now, we’ll miss the window.” But we had not talked it through properly, and it caused a lot of damage at home.

And at work, or with my own code, I’ve pushed an update without getting approval first. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.

press angles, and code changes, and sitting still feels like wasting the moment.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly feel like I'm the only one who can connect generative art, Mexican visual traditions, and the tech community in a way that could change how the city sees public art.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: When I start explaining the platform I just keep going—architecture, funding, cultural impact, deployment plan—and people say they can't get a word in, but to me it feels like the thoughts are arriving too fast to showcase.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / software_developer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… the main reason I came in is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s gotten really hard to finish even normal work tasks. Like, I’m a software developer, and I’ll be fixing some rendering bug—something that should just be me following the logic, checking the component, reading the stack trace—and then a Slack notification pops up, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain is off on a completely different track.\n\nAnd it doesn’t feel like ordinary distraction, exactly. It’s like each little thing pulls me into a whole new idea before I’ve finished the line of code I was writing. I’ll realize I’ve abandoned the original problem and I’m thinking about redesigning some unrelated UI thing, or looking into why the siren sounded, or following a thought that felt important for a second. Then I look back and I’ve got half-finished code and I’m not even sure where I was going with it. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s really interfering with my ability to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus at home or in other situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. Work is where it’s most obvious because there’s a task in front of me and I can actually see the trail of unfinished things, but at home it happens too.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen to do something simple—make coffee, grab a glass, whatever—and then I’ll notice a reflection or a pattern of light on the counter, or my phone buzzes, or I hear something outside, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing. And then a few minutes later I’m standing there with the cabinet open and I don’t even remember exactly what I came in for.\n\nIt feels like the same thing as when I’m coding, honestly. I’ll be in the middle of one thought, and then some tiny thing pulls me into a whole other chain of ideas before I finish the first one. I keep thinking, “Okay, just stay with the task,” but it’s like my attention keeps getting yanked sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. There have been times where I’ll get this feeling that if I don’t move right now, the whole thing is going to fall apart or the opportunity will be gone, and then I just… act.\n\nThe clearest example is I spent money my wife and I hadn’t agreed on. It wasn’t like buying a random toy or something—it made sense to me at the time, very much. I bought GPUs, and printing materials, and paid for some promo video stuff because I was convinced the project needed to scale immediately and look professional immediately. In my head it wasn’t “spending,” it was more like, “If I don’t secure this now, we’ll miss the window.” But we had not talked it through properly, and it caused a lot of damage at home.\n\nAnd at work, or with my own code, I’ve pushed an update without getting approval first. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah. Definitely. I mean… that’s probably the part that’s been hardest to admit, because it’s not just “I’m distracted” in some private way. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.

showcase.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / software_developer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus at home or in other situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. Work is where it’s most obvious because there’s a task in front of me and I can actually see the trail of unfinished things, but at home it happens too.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen to do something simple—make coffee, grab a glass, whatever—and then I’ll notice a reflection or a pattern of light on the counter, or my phone buzzes, or I hear something outside, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing. And then a few minutes later I’m standing there with the cabinet open and I don’t even remember exactly what I came in for.\n\nIt feels like the same thing as when I’m coding, honestly. It wasn’t like buying a random toy or something—it made sense to me at the time, very much. I bought GPUs, and printing materials, and paid for some promo video stuff because I was convinced the project needed to scale immediately and look professional immediately. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.\n\nAt work, the big thing was that I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. I really thought I was preventing a delay, like I was fixing something that needed to be fixed immediately, but my employer didn’t see it that way—understandably, I guess. They put me on leave after that. So now it’s not just a performance issue or someone being annoyed with me. It’s actually disrupted my job.\n\nAnd at home… yeah, my wife has been really upset. She says she doesn’t feel like she knows what I’m going to do next, especially financially or when I get locked onto an idea. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, overwhelmed is probably the right word. It’s like the anxiety comes after I realize I’ve lost the thread again.

What brought me in, really, is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.

And then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.

So yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.

pause.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I've been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, mock-planning installations, and trying to recruit other developers at all hours because the project feels bigger than my regular job now.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n  description: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.\n  manifestation: I spent money we hadn't agreed on for GPUs, print materials, and promo videos, and I pushed an update without approval because waiting for another review cycle felt like letting the whole opportunity die.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Before this whole showcase push, I wasn't reading secret museum or investor messages into posts and emails; that started only after I got so energized and consumed by the project.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My employer put me on leave after I bypassed approval and deployed changes on my own, and at home my wife says the spending and arguments have made things feel unstable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three months, since my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / software_developer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. There have been times where I’ll get this feeling that if I don’t move right now, the whole thing is going to fall apart or the opportunity will be gone, and then I just… act.\n\nThe clearest example is I spent money my wife and I hadn’t agreed on. It wasn’t like buying a random toy or something—it made sense to me at the time, very much. I bought GPUs, and printing materials, and paid for some promo video stuff because I was convinced the project needed to scale immediately and look professional immediately. In my head it wasn’t “spending,” it was more like, “If I don’t secure this now, we’ll miss the window.” But we had not talked it through properly, and it caused a lot of damage at home.\n\nAnd at work, or with my own code, I’ve pushed an update without getting approval first. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. I mean… that’s probably the part that’s been hardest to admit, because it’s not just “I’m distracted” in some private way. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.\n\nAt work, the big thing was that I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. I really thought I was preventing a delay, like I was fixing something that needed to be fixed immediately, but my employer didn’t see it that way—understandably, I guess. They put me on leave after that. So now it’s not just a performance issue or someone being annoyed with me. It’s actually disrupted my job.\n\nAnd at home… yeah, my wife has been really upset. The spending I mentioned, and the arguments around it, have made things feel unstable between us. She says she doesn’t feel like she knows what I’m going to do next, especially financially or when I get locked onto an idea. I keep trying to explain that, in the moment, it feels logical to me, but that doesn’t really undo the stress it puts on her. So yes, it’s caused real problems with my family and at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus or complete a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, overwhelmed is probably the right word. It’s like the anxiety comes after I realize I’ve lost the thread again.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.\n\nAnd then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and impulsivity?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes. I mean, pretty clearly at this point. It’s not just that I’m having a hard time concentrating and then I feel annoyed with myself. It’s changing the shape of my life, if that makes sense.

My employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. At the time I felt like I was solving a problem, like I was cutting through unnecessary delay, but the consequence was very real. I’m not working right now because of that decision, and that affects my routine, my confidence, money, everything.

And at home it’s been… tense. My wife has said the spending and the arguments around it have made things feel unstable. That word really stuck with me, because I don’t want our home to feel unstable. I keep thinking I can explain my reasoning and then it’ll make sense, but it doesn’t really fix the impact on her.

So yeah, overall quality of life—definitely. Work feels uncertain, home feels strained, and I feel like I’m constantly trying to clean up after choices I made when they seemed completely reasonable in the moment.

It’s not exactly all the time, no. It kind of comes in waves, or at least that’s how I experience it. But the bigger change—the period where it became noticeable and disruptive—has really been these last three months.

I keep anchoring it to the Glendale showcase because that’s when it started feeling different. So I wouldn’t say every single minute is equally bad, but the pattern has been there for about three months now, since Glendale became a thing.

I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.\n\nAt work, the big thing was that I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. I really thought I was preventing a delay, like I was fixing something that needed to be fixed immediately, but my employer didn’t see it that way—understandably, I guess. They put me on leave after that. So now it’s not just a performance issue or someone being annoyed with me. It’s actually disrupted my job.\n\nAnd at home… yeah, my wife has been really upset. She says she doesn’t feel like she knows what I’m going to do next, especially financially or when I get locked onto an idea. I keep trying to explain that, in the moment, it feels logical to me, but that doesn’t really undo the stress it puts on her. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, but people have told me I’m doing it.

It happens especially when I start talking about the platform. I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.

My wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. And I know that sounds bad, because conversation is supposed to go back and forth, but from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to steamroll anyone. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.

So yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.

Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that makes it hardest to dismiss as just being “stressed” or having a busy mind.

At work, it had a real consequence. I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own, and I know saying it like that sounds reckless. That really bothers me, because I don’t want her to feel like she can’t trust what’s happening in our own house. I keep trying to explain why I made certain decisions, but explaining doesn’t really undo the stress it’s caused.

So yes, my relationships and daily activities have changed a lot. Work is on hold, home feels tense, and I feel like I’m trying to repair damage from choices that, in the moment, didn’t feel wrong to me.

Like, I’m a software developer, and I’ll be fixing some rendering bug—something that should just be me following the logic, checking the component, reading the stack trace—and then a Slack notification pops up, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain is off on a completely different track.\n\nAnd it doesn’t feel like ordinary distraction, exactly. It’s like each little thing pulls me into a whole new idea before I’ve finished the line of code I was writing. I’ll realize I’ve abandoned the original problem and I’m thinking about redesigning some unrelated UI thing, or looking into why the siren sounded, or following a thought that felt important for a second. Then I look back and I’ve got half-finished code and I’m not even sure where I was going with it. That’s what made me think I probably needed to talk to someone, because it’s really interfering with my ability to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus at home or in other situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just at work. Work is where it’s most obvious because there’s a task in front of me and I can actually see the trail of unfinished things, but at home it happens too.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen to do something simple—make coffee, grab a glass, whatever—and then I’ll notice a reflection or a pattern of light on the counter, or my phone buzzes, or I hear something outside, and suddenly I’m thinking about some completely different thing. And then a few minutes later I’m standing there with the cabinet open and I don’t even remember exactly what I came in for.\n\nIt feels like the same thing as when I’m coding, honestly. I’ll be in the middle of one thought, and then some tiny thing pulls me into a whole other chain of ideas before I finish the first one. I keep thinking, “Okay, just stay with the task,” but it’s like my attention keeps getting yanked sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like admitting that, but yes. There have been times where I’ll get this feeling that if I don’t move right now, the whole thing is going to fall apart or the opportunity will be gone, and then I just… act.\n\nThe clearest example is I spent money my wife and I hadn’t agreed on. It wasn’t like buying a random toy or something—it made sense to me at the time, very much. I bought GPUs, and printing materials, and paid for some promo video stuff because I was convinced the project needed to scale immediately and look professional immediately. In my head it wasn’t “spending,” it was more like, “If I don’t secure this now, we’ll miss the window.” But we had not talked it through properly, and it caused a lot of damage at home.\n\nAnd at work, or with my own code, I’ve pushed an update without getting approval first. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. I mean… that’s probably the part that’s been hardest to admit, because it’s not just “I’m distracted” in some private way. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.\n\nAt work, the big thing was that I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. I really thought I was preventing a delay, like I was fixing something that needed to be fixed immediately, but my employer didn’t see it that way—understandably, I guess. They put me on leave after that. So now it’s not just a performance issue or someone being annoyed with me. It’s actually disrupted my job.\n\nAnd at home… yeah, my wife has been really upset. The spending I mentioned, and the arguments around it, have made things feel unstable between us. She says she doesn’t feel like she knows what I’m going to do next, especially financially or when I get locked onto an idea. I keep trying to explain that, in the moment, it feels logical to me, but that doesn’t really undo the stress it puts on her. So yes, it’s caused real problems with my family and at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus or complete a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, overwhelmed is probably the right word. It’s like the anxiety comes after I realize I’ve lost the thread again.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.\n\nAnd then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three months, I think. The timing is pretty clear to me because it started around when my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I could get intense about projects, sure, but this level of not being able to stay on one track—and making these snap decisions because everything felt urgent—that really picked up after the showcase thing happened. It was like once Glendale became real, my brain started treating everything like it was on a countdown. So… yeah, roughly three months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus and impulsivity are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, pretty clearly at this point. It’s not just that I’m having a hard time concentrating and then I feel annoyed with myself. It’s changing the shape of my life, if that makes sense.\n\nMy employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. At the time I felt like I was solving a problem, like I was cutting through unnecessary delay, but the consequence was very real. I’m not working right now because of that decision, and that affects my routine, my confidence, money, everything.\n\nAnd at home it’s been… tense. My wife has said the spending and the arguments around it have made things feel unstable. That word really stuck with me, because I don’t want our home to feel unstable. I keep thinking I can explain my reasoning and then it’ll make sense, but it doesn’t really fix the impact on her.\n\nSo yeah, overall quality of life—definitely. Work feels uncertain, home feels strained, and I feel like I’m constantly trying to clean up after choices I made when they seemed completely reasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity all the time, or are there periods where they are better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly all the time, no. It kind of comes in waves, or at least that’s how I experience it. But the bigger change—the period where it became noticeable and disruptive—has really been these last three months.\n\nI keep anchoring it to the Glendale showcase because that’s when it started feeling different. Before my platform was selected, I had stressful days and distracted days like anyone else, but after that, it was like my focus got much less reliable. Some hours are better than others, and if there’s less going on around me I can sometimes stay with a task longer, but overall since then it’s been much worse than my normal baseline.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say every single minute is equally bad, but the pattern has been there for about three months now, since Glendale became a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, but people have told me I’m doing it.\n\nIt happens especially when I start talking about the platform. I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.\n\nMy wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. And I know that sounds bad, because conversation is supposed to go back and forth, but from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to steamroll anyone. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships or daily activities due to your difficulty with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that makes it hardest to dismiss as just being “stressed” or having a busy mind.\n\nAt work, it had a real consequence. I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own, and I know saying it like that sounds reckless. At the time, though, it felt like I was removing an obstacle, like the process was slowing down something important. But my employer didn’t see it that way, understandably, and they put me on leave. So now my daily routine is completely disrupted. I’m not getting up and going into the usual work structure, I’m not in the same role day to day, and there’s this constant uncertainty hanging over me.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things with my wife. The spending has become a major source of arguments, and she’s told me it makes things feel unstable. That really bothers me, because I don’t want her to feel like she can’t trust what’s happening in our own house. I keep trying to explain why I made certain decisions, but explaining doesn’t really undo the stress it’s caused.\n\nSo yes, my relationships and daily activities have changed a lot. Work is on hold, home feels tense, and I feel like I’m trying to repair damage from choices that, in the moment, didn’t feel wrong to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… if I had to separate the two, the thing that brought me in is more the focus part. The impulsive decisions are a problem too, obviously, but what feels most constant and unsettling is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore.

Like I’ll sit down to fix a rendering bug, something that should be very concrete—look at the component, trace the issue, change the line of code, test it—and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain has jumped tracks completely. And it doesn’t just distract me for a second. It pulls me into a whole new idea, like some other feature or design direction or connection I hadn’t thought of, and then I’m following that before I’ve finished the thing I was supposed to be doing.

So it’s not just procrastination. It feels like my attention gets hijacked. I’ll look back and realize the line of code I was editing is still sitting there unfinished, and I’m three thoughts away from where I started. That’s the part that makes me feel like something is really off.

I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.\n\nAnd then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Restless is a good word for it, but it’s not just like bouncing my leg because I’m bored. It feels more like there’s this constant current running through me.

I’ll wake up and my mind is already moving—features I should build, how the platform should look if it were shown publicly, what the exhibition layout could be, what angle would make the press understand it, what code changes need to happen first. It’s all there before I’ve even really started the day. So then when I sit down and try to focus on one task, even something simple, sitting still feels almost… wrong. Like I’m wasting the moment by only doing one thing.

I do fidget. I get up, pace around, open my laptop, close it, check something, sketch something, go back to the code, then remember another idea and want to capture that. My body feels like it’s trying to keep up with the planning happening in my head.

And if someone asks me to just sit and relax, that’s hard. I can physically sit there, but internally I’m not settled. I’m thinking, “I should be using this time. I should be moving this forward.” So yes, I’ve definitely felt more restless when I’m supposed to be still or focused.

I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.\n\nAnd then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three months, I think. The timing is pretty clear to me because it started around when my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I could get intense about projects, sure, but this level of not being able to stay on one track—and making these snap decisions because everything felt urgent—that really picked up after the showcase thing happened. It was like once Glendale became real, my brain started treating everything like it was on a countdown. So… yeah, roughly three months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus and impulsivity are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, pretty clearly at this point. It’s not just that I’m having a hard time concentrating and then I feel annoyed with myself. It’s changing the shape of my life, if that makes sense.\n\nMy employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. At the time I felt like I was solving a problem, like I was cutting through unnecessary delay, but the consequence was very real. I’m not working right now because of that decision, and that affects my routine, my confidence, money, everything.\n\nAnd at home it’s been… tense. My wife has said the spending and the arguments around it have made things feel unstable. That word really stuck with me, because I don’t want our home to feel unstable. I keep thinking I can explain my reasoning and then it’ll make sense, but it doesn’t really fix the impact on her.\n\nSo yeah, overall quality of life—definitely. Work feels uncertain, home feels strained, and I feel like I’m constantly trying to clean up after choices I made when they seemed completely reasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity all the time, or are there periods where they are better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly all the time, no. It kind of comes in waves, or at least that’s how I experience it. But the bigger change—the period where it became noticeable and disruptive—has really been these last three months.\n\nI keep anchoring it to the Glendale showcase because that’s when it started feeling different. Before my platform was selected, I had stressful days and distracted days like anyone else, but after that, it was like my focus got much less reliable. Some hours are better than others, and if there’s less going on around me I can sometimes stay with a task longer, but overall since then it’s been much worse than my normal baseline.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say every single minute is equally bad, but the pattern has been there for about three months now, since Glendale became a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, but people have told me I’m doing it.\n\nIt happens especially when I start talking about the platform. I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.\n\nMy wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. And I know that sounds bad, because conversation is supposed to go back and forth, but from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to steamroll anyone. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships or daily activities due to your difficulty with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that makes it hardest to dismiss as just being “stressed” or having a busy mind.\n\nAt work, it had a real consequence. I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own, and I know saying it like that sounds reckless. At the time, though, it felt like I was removing an obstacle, like the process was slowing down something important. But my employer didn’t see it that way, understandably, and they put me on leave. So now my daily routine is completely disrupted. I’m not getting up and going into the usual work structure, I’m not in the same role day to day, and there’s this constant uncertainty hanging over me.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things with my wife. The spending has become a major source of arguments, and she’s told me it makes things feel unstable. That really bothers me, because I don’t want her to feel like she can’t trust what’s happening in our own house. I keep trying to explain why I made certain decisions, but explaining doesn’t really undo the stress it’s caused.\n\nSo yes, my relationships and daily activities have changed a lot. Work is on hold, home feels tense, and I feel like I’m trying to repair damage from choices that, in the moment, didn’t feel wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is not being able to focus, or is it more about acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I think… if I had to separate the two, the thing that brought me in is more the focus part. The impulsive decisions are a problem too, obviously, but what feels most constant and unsettling is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to fix a rendering bug, something that should be very concrete—look at the component, trace the issue, change the line of code, test it—and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain has jumped tracks completely. And it doesn’t just distract me for a second. It pulls me into a whole new idea, like some other feature or design direction or connection I hadn’t thought of, and then I’m following that before I’ve finished the thing I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo it’s not just procrastination. It feels like my attention gets hijacked. I’ll look back and realize the line of code I was editing is still sitting there unfinished, and I’m three thoughts away from where I started. That’s the part that makes me feel like something is really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still or trying to focus on one task?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that makes me feel like something is really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still or trying to focus on one task?\n\nPatient: Yes. Restless is a good word for it, but it’s not just like bouncing my leg because I’m bored. It feels more like there’s this constant current running through me.\n\nI’ll wake up and my mind is already moving—features I should build, how the platform should look if it were shown publicly, what the exhibition layout could be, what angle would make the press understand it, what code changes need to happen first. It’s all there before I’ve even really started the day. So then when I sit down and try to focus on one task, even something simple, sitting still feels almost… wrong. Like I’m wasting the moment by only doing one thing.\n\nI do fidget. I get up, pace around, open my laptop, close it, check something, sketch something, go back to the code, then remember another idea and want to capture that. I can physically sit there, but internally I’m not settled. I’m thinking, “I should be using this time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly, because it doesn’t feel pleasant or voluntary, like I’m just drifting off. It’s more like my attention gets grabbed by something else and I’m suddenly not fully with the person anymore.

If someone is talking to me, especially if there’s anything else happening around us, I can lose the thread really fast. A Slack notification, some visual detail like a color pattern on the screen, a sound outside like a siren—something small will catch me, and then my mind jumps to a whole other idea. And once it jumps, it doesn’t just come back neatly. I’ll be thinking about a design change or a feature or some connection I suddenly see, and then I realize the person is still talking and I’ve missed part of what they said.

That’s actually what brought me in, more than anything. I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Even when I’m doing something concrete, like fixing a rendering bug, I’ll be halfway through a line of code and something pulls me away mentally before I’ve finished it. Then I come back and I’m like, “Wait, what was I doing?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want people to think I don’t care or I’m not listening. I am trying to listen. I just don’t stay anchored to it.

In my head it wasn’t “spending,” it was more like, “If I don’t secure this now, we’ll miss the window.” But we had not talked it through properly, and it caused a lot of damage at home.\n\nAnd at work, or with my own code, I’ve pushed an update without getting approval first. I knew there was supposed to be another review cycle, but waiting felt unbearable, like sitting there while the whole opportunity died in slow motion. So I pushed it. At the time it felt necessary, almost obvious. Looking back, I can see that I didn’t really think through the consequences or give other people a chance to weigh in.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. I mean… that’s probably the part that’s been hardest to admit, because it’s not just “I’m distracted” in some private way. It’s started affecting other people, and they’re reacting to it.\n\nAt work, the big thing was that I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. I really thought I was preventing a delay, like I was fixing something that needed to be fixed immediately, but my employer didn’t see it that way—understandably, I guess. They put me on leave after that. So now it’s not just a performance issue or someone being annoyed with me. It’s actually disrupted my job.\n\nAnd at home… yeah, my wife has been really upset. The spending I mentioned, and the arguments around it, have made things feel unstable between us. She says she doesn’t feel like she knows what I’m going to do next, especially financially or when I get locked onto an idea. I keep trying to explain that, in the moment, it feels logical to me, but that doesn’t really undo the stress it puts on her. So yes, it’s caused real problems with my family and at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to focus or complete a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, overwhelmed is probably the right word. It’s like the anxiety comes after I realize I’ve lost the thread again.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be fixing something very specific, like a rendering bug, where I know exactly what line of code I’m supposed to be looking at, and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some weird color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain jumps to a completely different idea. Not just a quick distraction—more like I’m pulled into a whole new branch of thought before I’ve finished the original thing.\n\nAnd then I come back and I’m staring at the code thinking, “Wait, what was I doing?” That’s when I start feeling tense, because it keeps happening, and I can feel time slipping away. It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three months, I think. The timing is pretty clear to me because it started around when my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I could get intense about projects, sure, but this level of not being able to stay on one track—and making these snap decisions because everything felt urgent—that really picked up after the showcase thing happened. It was like once Glendale became real, my brain started treating everything like it was on a countdown. So… yeah, roughly three months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus and impulsivity are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, pretty clearly at this point. It’s not just that I’m having a hard time concentrating and then I feel annoyed with myself. It’s changing the shape of my life, if that makes sense.\n\nMy employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. At the time I felt like I was solving a problem, like I was cutting through unnecessary delay, but the consequence was very real. I’m not working right now because of that decision, and that affects my routine, my confidence, money, everything.\n\nAnd at home it’s been… tense. My wife has said the spending and the arguments around it have made things feel unstable. That word really stuck with me, because I don’t want our home to feel unstable. I keep thinking I can explain my reasoning and then it’ll make sense, but it doesn’t really fix the impact on her.\n\nSo yeah, overall quality of life—definitely. Work feels uncertain, home feels strained, and I feel like I’m constantly trying to clean up after choices I made when they seemed completely reasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity all the time, or are there periods where they are better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly all the time, no. It kind of comes in waves, or at least that’s how I experience it. But the bigger change—the period where it became noticeable and disruptive—has really been these last three months.\n\nI keep anchoring it to the Glendale showcase because that’s when it started feeling different. Before my platform was selected, I had stressful days and distracted days like anyone else, but after that, it was like my focus got much less reliable. Some hours are better than others, and if there’s less going on around me I can sometimes stay with a task longer, but overall since then it’s been much worse than my normal baseline.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say every single minute is equally bad, but the pattern has been there for about three months now, since Glendale became a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, but people have told me I’m doing it.\n\nIt happens especially when I start talking about the platform. I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.\n\nMy wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships or daily activities due to your difficulty with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. The impulsive decisions are a problem too, obviously, but what feels most constant and unsettling is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to fix a rendering bug, something that should be very concrete—look at the component, trace the issue, change the line of code, test it—and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain has jumped tracks completely. It’s actually not only in quiet environments. I mean, quiet can be hard too, because then there’s nothing external to sort of hold me in place, if that makes sense. But the thing that really brought me in is that my attention feels fragile almost anywhere now.

Like, I’ll be trying to fix a rendering bug, which should be very specific and contained. I know what I’m supposed to do: trace the component, look at the line of code, test the behavior. But then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or even a siren outside goes by, and it’s like my brain just gets pulled sideways. Not just “oh, that distracted me for a second,” but it pulls me into a whole different idea before I’ve even finished the line I was working on.

And then I’ll realize I’m no longer solving the original problem at all. I’m thinking about some design direction or some other feature or some connection that suddenly feels important. So I guess low-stimulation environments don’t necessarily protect me from it. The bigger issue is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore.

It’s frustrating because the task might be simple, or at least it should be simple, but I can’t hold the shape of it in my head long enough to complete it cleanly.\n\nSo yes, I get anxious, but it’s not always panic exactly. It’s more this pressure, like I’m trying to grab onto one thought and it keeps sliding out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s been about three months, I think. The timing is pretty clear to me because it started around when my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I could get intense about projects, sure, but this level of not being able to stay on one track—and making these snap decisions because everything felt urgent—that really picked up after the showcase thing happened. It was like once Glendale became real, my brain started treating everything like it was on a countdown. So… yeah, roughly three months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus and impulsivity are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s changing the shape of my life, if that makes sense.\n\nMy employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own. Work feels uncertain, home feels strained, and I feel like I’m constantly trying to clean up after choices I made when they seemed completely reasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity all the time, or are there periods where they are better or worse?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly all the time, no. It kind of comes in waves, or at least that’s how I experience it. But the bigger change—the period where it became noticeable and disruptive—has really been these last three months.\n\nI keep anchoring it to the Glendale showcase because that’s when it started feeling different. Before my platform was selected, I had stressful days and distracted days like anyone else, but after that, it was like my focus got much less reliable. Some hours are better than others, and if there’s less going on around me I can sometimes stay with a task longer, but overall since then it’s been much worse than my normal baseline.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say every single minute is equally bad, but the pattern has been there for about three months now, since Glendale became a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, but people have told me I’m doing it.\n\nIt happens especially when I start talking about the platform. I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.\n\nMy wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. And I know that sounds bad, because conversation is supposed to go back and forth, but from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to steamroll anyone. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships or daily activities due to your difficulty with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that makes it hardest to dismiss as just being “stressed” or having a busy mind.\n\nAt work, it had a real consequence. I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own, and I know saying it like that sounds reckless. At the time, though, it felt like I was removing an obstacle, like the process was slowing down something important. But my employer didn’t see it that way, understandably, and they put me on leave. So now my daily routine is completely disrupted. I’m not getting up and going into the usual work structure, I’m not in the same role day to day, and there’s this constant uncertainty hanging over me.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things with my wife. The spending has become a major source of arguments, and she’s told me it makes things feel unstable. That really bothers me, because I don’t want her to feel like she can’t trust what’s happening in our own house. I keep trying to explain why I made certain decisions, but explaining doesn’t really undo the stress it’s caused.\n\nSo yes, my relationships and daily activities have changed a lot. Work is on hold, home feels tense, and I feel like I’m trying to repair damage from choices that, in the moment, didn’t feel wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is not being able to focus, or is it more about acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I think… if I had to separate the two, the thing that brought me in is more the focus part. The impulsive decisions are a problem too, obviously, but what feels most constant and unsettling is that I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to fix a rendering bug, something that should be very concrete—look at the component, trace the issue, change the line of code, test it—and then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or there’s a siren outside, and suddenly my brain has jumped tracks completely. And it doesn’t just distract me for a second. It pulls me into a whole new idea, like some other feature or design direction or connection I hadn’t thought of, and then I’m following that before I’ve finished the thing I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo it’s not just procrastination. It feels like my attention gets hijacked. I’ll look back and realize the line of code I was editing is still sitting there unfinished, and I’m three thoughts away from where I started. That’s the part that makes me feel like something is really off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still or trying to focus on one task?\n\nPatient: Yes. Restless is a good word for it, but it’s not just like bouncing my leg because I’m bored. It feels more like there’s this constant current running through me.\n\nI’ll wake up and my mind is already moving—features I should build, how the platform should look if it were shown publicly, what the exhibition layout could be, what angle would make the press understand it, what code changes need to happen first. It’s all there before I’ve even really started the day. So then when I sit down and try to focus on one task, even something simple, sitting still feels almost… wrong. Like I’m wasting the moment by only doing one thing.\n\nI do fidget. I get up, pace around, open my laptop, close it, check something, sketch something, go back to the code, then remember another idea and want to capture that. My body feels like it’s trying to keep up with the planning happening in my head.\n\nAnd if someone asks me to just sit and relax, that’s hard. I can physically sit there, but internally I’m not settled. I’m thinking, “I should be using this time. I should be moving this forward.” So yes, I’ve definitely felt more restless when I’m supposed to be still or focused.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or having difficulty staying engaged when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly, because it doesn’t feel pleasant or voluntary, like I’m just drifting off. It’s more like my attention gets grabbed by something else and I’m suddenly not fully with the person anymore.\n\nIf someone is talking to me, especially if there’s anything else happening around us, I can lose the thread really fast. A Slack notification, some visual detail like a color pattern on the screen, a sound outside like a siren—something small will catch me, and then my mind jumps to a whole other idea. And once it jumps, it doesn’t just come back neatly. I’ll be thinking about a design change or a feature or some connection I suddenly see, and then I realize the person is still talking and I’ve missed part of what they said.\n\nThat’s actually what brought me in, more than anything. I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Even when I’m doing something concrete, like fixing a rendering bug, I’ll be halfway through a line of code and something pulls me away mentally before I’ve finished it. Then I come back and I’m like, “Wait, what was I doing?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t want people to think I don’t care or I’m not listening. I am trying to listen. I just don’t stay anchored to it.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Determine if the patient's symptoms are consistent with a pattern of inattention or hyperactivity,\" \"question\": \"Do you find that you are more easily distracted or have greater difficulty focusing when you are in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\"}\n\nPatient: It’s actually not only in quiet environments. I mean, quiet can be hard too, because then there’s nothing external to sort of hold me in place, if that makes sense. But the thing that really brought me in is that my attention feels fragile almost anywhere now.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to fix a rendering bug, which should be very specific and contained. I know what I’m supposed to do: trace the component, look at the line of code, test the behavior. But then a Slack ping comes in, or I notice some color pattern on the screen, or even a siren outside goes by, and it’s like my brain just gets pulled sideways. Not just “oh, that distracted me for a second,” but it pulls me into a whole different idea before I’ve even finished the line I was working on.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’m no longer solving the original problem at all. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes. That’s been happening a lot. It’s like I’m running on this constant current, and my body doesn’t really know how to shut it off.

I’ll wake up and I’m already planning things before I’m even fully out of bed—features I should build, how the whole thing should look if it were shown somewhere, what the exhibition layout would be, what angle would make the press understand it, what code changes need to happen first. It’s all stacked up in my head immediately. So by the time I’m supposed to sit still, it already feels like I’m behind.

And sitting still for a long time is really uncomfortable because it feels like I’m wasting the moment. Like, if I’m just sitting there, part of me is thinking, “Why am I not doing something with this? Why am I not moving this forward?” So I’ll shift around, get up, pace, go back to the laptop, jot something down, then remember another thing and want to act on that too.

It’s not just tapping my foot once in a while. It feels more urgent than that, like stillness is almost irritating because there’s so much momentum inside me.

I’ll begin with what seems like one simple explanation, and then suddenly I’m covering the architecture, the funding model, the deployment plan, the cultural impact—how it could change access, who needs to hear about it, why the timing matters—and I can feel all of it lining up in my head at once. It’s like if I don’t say it immediately, I’m going to lose the thread, or the opportunity, or something important.\n\nMy wife and coworkers have said they can’t get a word in when I’m like that. And I know that sounds bad, because conversation is supposed to go back and forth, but from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to steamroll anyone. It feels more like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. By the time someone is halfway through a question, I already have three answers and a whole explanation forming, so I jump in.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do interrupt or talk over people more than I used to, especially when I’m explaining the platform or trying to convince someone of the plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your relationships or daily activities due to your difficulty with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s probably the part that makes it hardest to dismiss as just being “stressed” or having a busy mind.\n\nAt work, it had a real consequence. I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes on my own, and I know saying it like that sounds reckless. I’m not getting up and going into the usual work structure, I’m not in the same role day to day, and there’s this constant uncertainty hanging over me.\n\nAnd at home, it’s affected things with my wife. I keep trying to explain why I made certain decisions, but explaining doesn’t really undo the stress it’s caused.\n\nSo yes, my relationships and daily activities have changed a lot. And it doesn’t just distract me for a second. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I don’t always mean to, but I’ve been told I kind of take over conversations.

It happens most when I start explaining the platform I’m working on. I’ll start with one piece, like the architecture, and then before I’ve even finished that I’m already explaining the funding model, and then the cultural impact, and then how it would be deployed, and then why it matters, and how people would respond to it. In my head it all connects, so it feels like I’m just following the logic of it, but from the outside people say they can’t really get a word in.

And I do notice that sometimes someone will start to ask something or make a point, and I’ll jump in because I already see where it’s going—or I think I do—and I don’t want to lose the thought. It feels like the thoughts are arriving too fast to pause politely. If I stop, I’m afraid the whole chain of ideas will disappear or I’ll lose the momentum.

So, yes, waiting my turn has been hard. Not because I’m trying to be rude. It feels more like there’s this pressure behind the words, and once I start talking, it’s hard to leave space.

Yes. It’s not contained to one place, unfortunately. If it were just work, I could tell myself it was the project or the deadlines or the environment, but it’s showing up everywhere.

It’s been pretty consistently there for about three months now. I can place it because it really seemed to start after my platform was selected for the Glendale showcase.
